  "short_description": {
    "id": "Susanoo adalah kami Jepang yang kisahnya mencakup perselisihan di langit dan kemenangan atas ular Yamata no Orochi di Izumo.",
    "en": "Susanoo is a kami whose stories include conflict in heaven and victory over the serpent Yamata no Orochi in Izumo.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "susanoo-c01",
      "susanoo-c02",
      "susanoo-c06",
      "susanoo-c07"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Dalam Kojiki, Susanoo muncul dari hidung Izanagi ketika sang dewa menyucikan diri setelah kembali dari Yomi. Nihongi menyajikan asal-usul lain: Susanoo lahir dari Izanagi dan Izanami. Kedua kisah menempatkannya bersama Amaterasu dan Tsukuyomi sebagai tiga anak mulia.",
      "en": "In the Kojiki, Susanoo emerges from Izanagi’s nose during purification after his return from Yomi. The Nihongi offers another origin: Susanoo is born to Izanagi and Izanami. Both accounts place him alongside Amaterasu and Tsukuyomi among the three noble children.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "susanoo-c01",
        "susanoo-c03",
        "susanoo-c04"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Susanoo dikaitkan dengan angin dan hujan. Kisahnya menceritakan bahwa ia diusir setelah tindakannya membuat Amaterasu bersembunyi dalam gua langit. Di Izumo ia membunuh Yamata no Orochi dan memperoleh pedang Kusanagi; kisah kuil setempat menyebut ia kemudian menikahi putri pasangan yang diselamatkannya.",
      "en": "Susanoo is associated with wind and rain. His story says he was banished after his actions drove Amaterasu into a heavenly cave. In Izumo he slew Yamata no Orochi and acquired the sword Kusanagi; a local shrine account says he then married the rescued couple’s daughter.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "susanoo-c05",
        "susanoo-c06",
        "susanoo-c07",
        "susanoo-c08"
      ]
    }
  ],

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "susanoo-c01",
      "source_id": "susanoo-s1",
      "quote": "A kami introduced by (Kojiki) as having come into being from the nose of Izanagi no mikoto as he was performing ablutions (misogi) to rid himself of pollution encountered while in the underworld (Yomi).",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Menurut Kojiki, Susanoo adalah kami yang muncul dari hidung Izanagi ketika ia menyucikan diri sesudah mengunjungi Yomi.",
        "en": "According to the Kojiki, Susanoo is a kami who emerged from Izanagi’s nose during purification after a visit to Yomi."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "susanoo-c02",
      "source_id": "susanoo-s2",
      "quote": "The Izumo region and Izumo Oyashiro Shrine feature prominently in several of Japan’s earliest myths.",
      "locator": "The Land of Myths",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah Izumo termasuk mitos awal Jepang.",
        "en": "The stories of Izumo feature in Japan’s early myths."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "susanoo-c03",
      "source_id": "susanoo-s1",
      "quote": "In Nihongi, however, Susanoo is described as being produced by Izanagi and Izanami",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Nihongi menyebut Susanoo lahir dari Izanagi dan Izanami.",
        "en": "The Nihongi says Susanoo was born to Izanagi and Izanami."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "susanoo-c04",
      "source_id": "susanoo-s1",
      "quote": "Both accounts describe Susanoo as one of a set of three kami known as the \"three noble children,\" the other being Amaterasu and Tsukuyomi",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kedua sumber menempatkan Susanoo bersama Amaterasu dan Tsukuyomi sebagai tiga anak mulia.",
        "en": "Both accounts place Susanoo with Amaterasu and Tsukuyomi among the three noble children."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "susanoo-c05",
      "source_id": "susanoo-s1",
      "quote": "Susanoo has been associated with wind and rain, but many elements of his personality remain unclear.",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Susanoo dikaitkan dengan angin dan hujan.",
        "en": "Susanoo has been associated with wind and rain."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "susanoo-c06",
      "source_id": "susanoo-s1",
      "quote": "Susanoo ran amok and caused Amaterasu to hide herself away in the heavenly cave, for which offense Susanoo was again banished.",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Susanoo diusir setelah menyebabkan Amaterasu bersembunyi di gua langit.",
        "en": "Susanoo was banished after causing Amaterasu to hide in the heavenly cave."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "susanoo-c07",
      "source_id": "susanoo-s1",
      "quote": "Susanoo defeated the serpent Yamata no orochi, received the sword \"Kusanagi\"",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Susanoo mengalahkan Yamata no Orochi dan memperoleh pedang Kusanagi.",
        "en": "Susanoo defeated Yamata no Orochi and received the sword Kusanagi."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "susanoo-c08",
      "source_id": "susanoo-s2",
      "quote": "Susanoo agreed to slay the beast in exchange for permission to marry their daughter.",
      "locator": "The Land of Myths",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Susanoo setuju membunuh ular itu agar dapat menikahi putri pasangan tersebut.",
        "en": "Susanoo agreed to slay the serpent in exchange for marrying the couple’s daughter."
      }
    }
  ],

  "countries": null,

  "traits": [],
  "short_description": {
    "id": "Izanagi adalah dewa Shinto yang dalam kisah penciptaan Jepang membentuk pulau-pulau bersama Izanami.",
    "en": "Izanagi is a Shinto deity who forms islands with Izanami in a creation myth.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "izanagi-c01",
      "izanagi-c02"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Kisah penciptaan menggambarkan Izanagi dan Izanami berdiri di Jembatan Langit lalu mengaduk laut dengan tombak. Tetesan dari tombak itu menjadi sebuah pulau. Setelah Izanami meninggal saat melahirkan dewa api, Izanagi pergi menyusulnya ke Yomi.",
      "en": "The creation story places Izanagi and Izanami on the Floating Bridge of Heaven, stirring the sea with a spear. Drops from the spear become an island. After Izanami dies giving birth to the fire god, Izanagi follows her to Yomi.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "izanagi-c02",
        "izanagi-c03",
        "izanagi-c04",
        "izanagi-c05"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Izanagi melarikan diri dari Yomi dan menutup pintu keluarnya dengan batu besar. Ia lalu mandi untuk menyucikan diri. Kisah ini menyebut Amaterasu lahir ketika ia membasuh mata kiri, Tsukuyomi dari mata kanan, dan Susanoo dari hidung.",
      "en": "Izanagi flees Yomi and closes its exit with a large boulder. He then bathes to purify himself. The story says Amaterasu is born when he washes his left eye, Tsukuyomi from his right eye, and Susanoo from his nose.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "izanagi-c06",
        "izanagi-c07",
        "izanagi-c08"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,
  "story_mode": null,
  "did_you_know": null,
  "abilities": [],
  "weaknesses": [],
  "variants": [],
  "stories": [],
  "places": [],
  "timeline": [],
  "relations": [],

  "learning_questions": [],
  "images": [],
  "sources": [
    {
      "id": "izanagi-s1",
      "url": "https://www.worldhistory.org/Izanami_and_Izanagi/",
      "title": "Izanami and Izanagi",
      "author": "Mark Cartwright",
      "publisher": "World History Encyclopedia",
      "published": "2012-12-06",
      "language": "en",
      "type": "encyclopedia",
      "accessed": "2026-09-30"
    },
    {
      "id": "izanagi-s2",
      "url": "https://d-museum.kokugakuin.ac.jp/eos/detail/?id=9385",
      "title": "Izanagi",
      "author": "Kadoya Atsushi",
      "publisher": "Encyclopedia of Shinto, Kokugakuin University",
      "published": null,
      "language": "en",
      "type": "encyclopedia",
      "accessed": "2026-09-30"
    }
  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "izanagi-c01",
      "source_id": "izanagi-s1",
      "quote": "Izanami ('she who invites') and Izanagi ('he who invites') are the primordial gods of the Shinto religion who are believed to have created the islands of Japan",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Izanagi adalah dewa purba Shinto yang dipercaya ikut menciptakan pulau-pulau Jepang.",
        "en": "Izanagi is a primordial Shinto god believed to have helped create the islands of Japan."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "izanagi-c02",
      "source_id": "izanagi-s2",
      "quote": "According to Kojiki and Nihongi, one of the two kami (together with his consort Izanami) principally responsible for the formation of the world.",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Menurut Kojiki dan Nihongi, Izanagi bersama Izanami berperan utama dalam pembentukan dunia.",
        "en": "According to the Kojiki and Nihongi, Izanagi and Izanami are central to the world’s formation."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "izanagi-c03",
      "source_id": "izanagi-s2",
      "quote": "According to the myth, the two kami first stood on the \"floating bridge of heaven\" and used a spear to stir the sea below, whereupon the brine dripping from the spear's point congealed and formed the island of Onogoro.",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kedua kami berdiri di jembatan langit dan mengaduk laut dengan tombak; tetesannya menjadi Pulau Onogoro.",
        "en": "The two kami stand on a heavenly bridge and stir the sea with a spear; its drips form Onogoro Island."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "izanagi-c04",
      "source_id": "izanagi-s2",
      "quote": "Izanami died as the result of giving birth to the kami of fire",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Izanami meninggal akibat melahirkan dewa api.",
        "en": "Izanami died as a result of giving birth to the fire kami."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "izanagi-c05",
      "source_id": "izanagi-s1",
      "quote": "Izanagi, no doubt unable to live without his beloved wife, rashly followed her down into the underworld or Yomi",
      "locator": "Izanami's Death & Revenge",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Izanagi mengikuti Izanami ke Yomi.",
        "en": "Izanagi followed Izanami to Yomi."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "izanagi-c06",
      "source_id": "izanagi-s1",
      "quote": "On finally reaching the outside world, Izanagi blocked the entrance to Yomi with an enormous stone. Fortunate to escape unharmed from such a terrible place of darkness the god had to perform a cleansing ritual in the river Woto to rid himself of the impurities of the underworld.",
      "locator": "Birth of the Shinto Gods",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Izanagi menutup pintu Yomi dengan batu besar dan menjalani penyucian diri di sungai.",
        "en": "Izanagi sealed Yomi with a large stone and performed river purification."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "izanagi-c07",
      "source_id": "izanagi-s1",
      "quote": "It was during these rituals that various gods were born: Amaterasu, the sun goddess, when he washed his left eye, Tsuki-yomi, the god of the moon, when he washed his right eye, Susanoo (or Take-haya-Susa-no-wo), the storm god, when he washed his nose",
      "locator": "Birth of the Shinto Gods",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Saat penyucian, Amaterasu lahir dari mata kiri, Tsuki-yomi dari mata kanan, dan Susanoo dari hidung Izanagi.",
        "en": "During purification, Amaterasu emerges from his left eye, Tsuki-yomi from his right, and Susanoo from his nose."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "izanagi-c08",
      "source_id": "izanagi-s2",
      "quote": "the \"three noble children\" Amaterasu, Tsukuyomi, and Susanoo were also produced at this time",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Amaterasu, Tsukuyomi, dan Susanoo dikenal sebagai tiga anak mulia.",
        "en": "Amaterasu, Tsukuyomi, and Susanoo are known as the three noble children."
      }
    }
  ],
  "conflicts": [],

  "short_description": {
    "id": "Yōkai adalah golongan makhluk dan gejala gaib dalam cerita rakyat Jepang, termasuk roh dan monster.",
    "en": "Yōkai is a broad category of supernatural beings and phenomena in Japanese folklore, including spirits and monsters.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "yokai-c01",
      "yokai-c03"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Yōkai mencakup roh, monster, dan berbagai gejala gaib dalam cerita rakyat Jepang. Sebagian digambarkan mirip manusia dengan ciri yang berubah, sedangkan yang lain menyerupai binatang atau benda rumah tangga. Istilah ini luas dan tidak memiliki padanan tunggal dalam bahasa Inggris.",
      "en": "Yōkai includes spirits, monsters, and many supernatural phenomena in Japanese folklore. Some are shown as humanlike figures with altered features, while others resemble animals or household objects. The term is broad and lacks a single English equivalent.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "yokai-c01",
        "yokai-c02",
        "yokai-c03",
        "yokai-c04"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Dalam seni, yōkai muncul pada gulungan gambar dan cetakan kayu. Yōkai yang dahulu membangkitkan rasa takut belakangan juga ditampilkan sebagai sosok yang lebih bersahabat. Tema yōkai kini hadir dalam mainan dan film.",
      "en": "In art, yōkai appears in picture scrolls and woodblock prints. Once feared, yōkai has also been presented as a friendlier presence. Yōkai now appears in toys and films.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "yokai-c05",
        "yokai-c06",
        "yokai-c07"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "yokai-c01",
      "source_id": "yokai-s1",
      "quote": "Yōkai (妖怪) are creatures and phenomena of Japanese folklore including spirits, monsters, and just about all things supernatural.",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Yōkai adalah makhluk dan gejala gaib dalam cerita rakyat Jepang, termasuk roh dan monster.",

      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yokai-c02",
      "source_id": "yokai-s2",
      "quote": "In Japanese folklore, yōkai (妖怪) refers to legendary ghosts, monsters, and spirits.",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dalam cerita rakyat Jepang, yōkai mencakup hantu, monster, dan roh legendaris.",
        "en": "In Japanese folklore, yōkai includes legendary ghosts, monsters, and spirits."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yokai-c03",
      "source_id": "yokai-s1",
      "quote": "it is a broad and vague term with no real English translation",
      "locator": "Yōkai and Amabie",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Yōkai adalah istilah luas yang tidak memiliki padanan bahasa Inggris yang tepat.",
        "en": "Yōkai is a broad term with no precise English equivalent."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yokai-c04",
      "source_id": "yokai-s2",
      "quote": "some resembling humans, for example, with altered features such as a long neck or three eyes. Others looked like strange animals, plants, insects, or household goods.",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Yōkai dapat digambarkan menyerupai manusia berciri berubah, binatang, tumbuhan, serangga, atau benda rumah tangga.",
        "en": "Yōkai may be depicted as humans with altered features, animals, plants, insects, or household objects."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yokai-c05",
      "source_id": "yokai-s3",
      "quote": "This exhibition introduces Japan’s yokai (Japanese folkloric imaginary monsters) culture that extends to the present day through picture scrolls and nishiki-e (a multicolored Japanese woodblock print)",
      "locator": "Exhibition introduction",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Budaya yōkai hadir dalam gulungan gambar dan cetakan kayu berwarna.",
        "en": "Yōkai culture appears in picture scrolls and multicolored woodblock prints."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yokai-c06",
      "source_id": "yokai-s3",
      "quote": "yokai seem to have gradually become less of a subject of fear and have come to be viewed as a more charming and friendly presence.",
      "locator": "Exhibition introduction",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Yōkai yang dahulu ditakuti juga kemudian dilihat sebagai sosok yang lebih ramah.",
        "en": "Yōkai, once feared, have also come to be viewed as friendlier presences."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yokai-c07",
      "source_id": "yokai-s3",
      "quote": "as well as a diverse range of media, including toys and films.",
      "locator": "Exhibition introduction",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Yōkai hadir dalam media seperti mainan dan film.",
        "en": "Yōkai appear in media including toys and films."
      }
    }
  ],

    "id": "Tsukuyomi adalah kami yang berkaitan dengan bulan dan malam.",
    "en": "Tsukuyomi is a kami associated with the moon and night.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "tsukuyomi-c01",
      "tsukuyomi-c02",

    ]

  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Kojiki menceritakan Tsukuyomi muncul saat Izanagi membasuh mata kanannya dalam upacara penyucian. Ia termasuk tiga anak mulia. Namanya dikaitkan dengan perhitungan fase bulan.",
      "en": "The Kojiki says Tsukuyomi emerged when Izanagi washed his right eye during purification. He is counted among the three noble children. His name is connected with reading the phases of the moon.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "tsukuyomi-c01",
        "tsukuyomi-c03",
        "tsukuyomi-c04"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Sumber-sumber lama berbeda mengenai wilayah yang dipercayakan kepadanya: laut menurut satu versi Nihongi, malam menurut Kojiki. Versi lain Nihongi mengisahkan Tsukuyomi membunuh dewi makanan Ukemochi, lalu Amaterasu menjauhinya; kisah itu menjelaskan pemisahan siang dan malam. Tsukuyomi juga dipuja di Tsukuyomi no Miya di Ise.",
      "en": "Older sources differ on his assigned realm: the sea in one Nihongi version, the night in the Kojiki. Another Nihongi version tells of Tsukuyomi killing the food deity Ukemochi, after which Amaterasu separates from him; the tale explains the division of day and night. Tsukuyomi is also worshiped at Tsukuyomi no Miya in Ise.",
      "claim_ids": [

    {
      "id": "tsukuyomi-c01",
      "source_id": "tsukuyomi-s1",
      "quote": "The second of Izanagi and Izanami's \"three noble children,\" and usually considered a male kami with rule over the night.",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Tsukuyomi adalah kami laki-laki yang dianggap berkuasa atas malam.",
        "en": "Tsukuyomi is a male kami regarded as ruling over the night."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "tsukuyomi-c02",
      "source_id": "tsukuyomi-s2",
      "quote": "moon kami in Japanese mythology and the Shinto religion.",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Tsukuyomi adalah kami bulan dalam mitologi Jepang dan agama Shinto.",
        "en": "Tsukuyomi is a moon kami in Japanese mythology and Shinto."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "tsukuyomi-c03",
      "source_id": "tsukuyomi-s1",
      "quote": "The name tsuku-yomi is thought to be originally related to the lunar calendar, and refers to the \"reading\" (yomu) of the phases of the \"moon\" (tsuki).",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "etymology",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Nama Tsukuyomi diduga berkaitan dengan pembacaan fase bulan dalam kalender lunar.",
        "en": "Tsukuyomi’s name is thought to relate to reading moon phases in the lunar calendar."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "tsukuyomi-c04",
      "source_id": "tsukuyomi-s1",
      "quote": "According to Kojiki and an \"alternate writing\" in Nihongi, Tsukuyomi came into being when Izanagi washed his right eye as he was undergoing ablution.",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Menurut Kojiki dan versi Nihongi, Tsukuyomi muncul saat Izanagi membasuh mata kanannya.",
        "en": "According to the Kojiki and one Nihongi version, Tsukuyomi emerged when Izanagi washed his right eye."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "tsukuyomi-c05",
      "source_id": "tsukuyomi-s1",
      "quote": "Tsukuyomi was entrusted by Izanagi with rule variously over the sea (Nihongi) or over the realm of night (Kojiki).",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Nihongi menempatkan Tsukuyomi atas laut, sedangkan Kojiki atas malam.",
        "en": "The Nihongi gives Tsukuyomi the sea, while the Kojiki gives him the night."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "tsukuyomi-c06",
      "source_id": "tsukuyomi-s1",
      "quote": "an \"alternate writing\" in Nihongi relates that Tsukuyomi originally resided together with Amaterasu in heaven, but after killing the kami of foods Ukemochi, he was condemned by Amaterasu as an \"evil kami\" and forced to live apart from the sun, resulting in the separation of day and night.",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Versi Nihongi menyatakan Tsukuyomi membunuh Ukemochi lalu dipisahkan dari matahari oleh Amaterasu, menjelaskan pemisahan siang dan malam.",
        "en": "A Nihongi version says Tsukuyomi killed Ukemochi and was separated from the sun by Amaterasu, explaining day and night."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "tsukuyomi-c07",
      "source_id": "tsukuyomi-s1",
      "quote": "Tsukuyomi is the object of worship (saijin) at the detached shrine (betsugū) Tsukuyomi no Miya of the Grand Shrines of Ise",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Tsukuyomi dipuja di Tsukuyomi no Miya di Ise.",
        "en": "Tsukuyomi is worshiped at Tsukuyomi no Miya in Ise."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "tsukuyomi-c08",
      "source_id": "tsukuyomi-s3",
      "quote": "Tsukiyomi-no-Mikoto is the deity of Tsukiyomi-no-miya / the Koutaijingu Betsugu.It is a younger brother of Amaterasu Omikami and is likened to the moon.",
      "locator": "Tsukiyomi no Miya",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Tsukuyomi dipuja di Tsukiyomi-no-miya dan dikaitkan dengan bulan.",
        "en": "Tsukuyomi is enshrined at Tsukiyomi-no-miya and associated with the moon."
      }
    }
  ],

  "short_description": {
    "id": "Tengu adalah yōkai dalam tradisi Jepang yang diasosiasikan dengan hutan pegunungan dan memiliki wujud manusia berciri burung.",
    "en": "Tengu are yōkai of Japanese tradition associated with mountain forests and depicted with human and birdlike features.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "tengu-c01",
      "tengu-c02",
      "tengu-c03",
      "tengu-c04"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Dalam kepercayaan rakyat Jepang, tengu ditempatkan di hutan pegunungan. Gambaran yang umum menunjukkan tubuh menyerupai manusia dan sayap. Karasu Tengu berkepala burung dengan paruh, sedangkan Ko no ha Tengu berwajah manusia dengan hidung sangat panjang.",
      "en": "In Japanese folk belief, tengu inhabit mountain woodlands. Common depictions give them humanlike bodies and wings. Karasu Tengu have bird heads and beaks, while Ko no ha Tengu have human faces with very long noses.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "tengu-c02",
        "tengu-c03",
        "tengu-c04"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Kisah tengu telah berubah dari masa ke masa. Cerita lama menggambarkan mereka sebagai musuh agama Buddha yang menipu pendeta. Mereka juga dikisahkan memiliki kekuatan luar biasa, dapat terbang, berubah bentuk, dan merasuki manusia. Meski sering digambarkan berbahaya, ada pula kisah tentang tengu yang melindungi orang atau kuil.",
      "en": "Tengu stories have changed over time. Older tales portray them as enemies of Buddhism who deceive priests. They are also credited with great strength, flight, changing shape, and possessing people. Although often depicted as dangerous, some accounts describe tengu protecting people or temples.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "tengu-c02",
        "tengu-c05",
        "tengu-c06",
        "tengu-c07",
        "tengu-c08"
      ]
    }

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "tengu-c01",
      "source_id": "tengu-s1",
      "quote": "They are considered a type of yōkai (supernatural being) or kami (god or spirit).",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Tengu dapat dianggap sebagai yōkai atau kami.",
        "en": "Tengu can be considered yōkai or kami."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "tengu-c02",
      "source_id": "tengu-s2",
      "quote": "a tengu, the embodiment of powerful and usually malevolent spirits who, in Japanese folk beliefs, inhabit the woodlands of high mountains.",
      "locator": "Object description",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Menurut kepercayaan rakyat Jepang, tengu menghuni hutan pegunungan tinggi.",
        "en": "In Japanese folk belief, tengu inhabit high mountain woodlands."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "tengu-c03",
      "source_id": "tengu-s2",
      "quote": "They are typically represented as having human bodies, wings, and sometimes talons in place of hands and feet.",
      "locator": "Object description",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Tengu biasanya digambarkan bertubuh manusia, bersayap, dan kadang bercakar.",
        "en": "Tengu are usually portrayed with human bodies, wings, and sometimes talons."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "tengu-c04",
      "source_id": "tengu-s2",
      "quote": "The Karasu Tengu has a birdlike head complete with a strong, sharp beak. The Ko no ha Tengu has a human face, distorted by an enormously long nose.",
      "locator": "Object description",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Karasu Tengu berkepala burung berparuh tajam, sedangkan Ko no ha Tengu berwajah manusia berhidung sangat panjang.",
        "en": "Karasu Tengu has a birdlike head and sharp beak; Ko no ha Tengu has a human face and very long nose."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "tengu-c05",
      "source_id": "tengu-s2",
      "quote": "They are credited with many powers, including supernatural strength and the ability to fly, to assume other forms, and to take over humans through possession.",
      "locator": "Object description",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Tengu dikisahkan memiliki kekuatan luar biasa, terbang, berubah wujud, dan merasuki manusia.",
        "en": "Tengu are credited with great strength, flight, shape-changing, and possessing humans."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "tengu-c06",
      "source_id": "tengu-s2",
      "quote": "Occasionally, however, tengu appear as benevolent spirits who protect certain individuals or temples.",
      "locator": "Object description",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Tengu kadang digambarkan sebagai roh baik yang melindungi orang atau kuil.",
        "en": "Tengu are sometimes described as benevolent spirits protecting people or temples."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "tengu-c07",
      "source_id": "tengu-s3",
      "quote": "portray tengu as enemies of Buddhism, setting fires at temples or tricking priests.",
      "locator": "tengu entry",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah lama menggambarkan tengu sebagai musuh agama Buddha yang menipu pendeta atau membakar kuil.",
        "en": "Older tales portray tengu as enemies of Buddhism who trick priests or burn temples."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "tengu-c08",
      "source_id": "tengu-s3",
      "quote": "For instance, tengu were long thought to abduct children, but by the Edo period they often were enlisted to aid in the search for missing children.",
      "locator": "tengu entry",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Citra tengu berubah; pada zaman Edo mereka juga diminta membantu mencari anak hilang.",
        "en": "Tengu’s image changed; by the Edo period they were also enlisted to help find missing children."
      }
    }
  ],

    "en": "Inari is a kami associated with rice, grain, and prosperity in Japan.",

      "inari-c01",
      "inari-c02",
      "inari-c06"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Dalam istilah Shinto, Inari disebut pelindung budidaya padi dan lima jenis biji-bijian. Kitab Shinto juga menyebutnya Uka-no-mitama-no-kami. Kuil Fushimi Inari Taisha menjelaskan bahwa lima tempat pemujaan utamanya mewakili berbagai keutamaan Inari Okami.",
      "en": "In Shinto terminology, Inari is a tutelary deity of rice cultivation and five grains. Shinto classics also call Inari Uka-no-mitama-no-kami. Fushimi Inari Taisha explains that five main enshrined deities embody different virtues of Inari Okami.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "inari-c01",
        "inari-c03",
        "inari-c04"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Tradisi Inari juga berhubungan dengan rubah. Pemuja Inari di berbagai daerah mengaitkannya dengan kesejahteraan perdagangan dan kebutuhan setempat. Kuil Fushimi Inari menjelaskan nama Inari sebagai bentuk pendek ungkapan lama yang berkaitan dengan panen padi.",
      "en": "Inari tradition is also associated with foxes. Worshipers in different regions connect Inari with commercial prosperity and local concerns. Fushimi Inari Shrine explains the name Inari as a shortened older expression relating to rice harvests.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "inari-c05",
        "inari-c06",
        "inari-c07"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,
  "story_mode": null,
  "did_you_know": null,

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "inari-c01",
      "source_id": "inari-s1",
      "quote": "A tutelary deity of rice cultivation and the five grains.",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Inari adalah dewa pelindung budidaya padi dan lima jenis biji-bijian.",
        "en": "Inari is a tutelary deity of rice cultivation and the five grains."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "inari-c02",
      "source_id": "inari-s2",
      "quote": "People throughout Japan pray to Inari Okami for these things",
      "locator": "Virtues of the Deity",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Orang di seluruh Jepang berdoa kepada Inari Okami.",
        "en": "People throughout Japan pray to Inari Okami."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "inari-c03",
      "source_id": "inari-s1",
      "quote": "In the Shinto classics Inari is desiguated as Uka-no-mitama-no-kami.",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kitab-kitab Shinto menyebut Inari Uka-no-mitama-no-kami.",
        "en": "Shinto classics call Inari Uka-no-mitama-no-kami."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "inari-c04",
      "source_id": "inari-s2",
      "quote": "These five pillars of Fushimi Inari Taisha are named for the great virtues of Inari Okami that they embody.",
      "locator": "Main Shrine Building",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Lima tempat pemujaan utama Fushimi Inari Taisha mewakili keutamaan Inari Okami.",
        "en": "Five enshrined deities at Fushimi Inari Taisha embody virtues of Inari Okami."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "inari-c05",
      "source_id": "inari-s3",
      "quote": "Inari faith also has a deep association with foxes.",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kepercayaan Inari berhubungan erat dengan rubah.",
        "en": "Inari belief has a close association with foxes."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "inari-c06",
      "source_id": "inari-s3",
      "quote": "In commercial regions, Inari was believed to be the kami who ensured commercial prosperity. In fishing villages Inari was the kami of fishing.",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Di daerah perdagangan, Inari dipercaya membawa kemakmuran usaha; di desa nelayan ia dikaitkan dengan perikanan.",
        "en": "In commercial regions Inari was linked to prosperity, and in fishing villages to fishing."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "inari-c07",
      "source_id": "inari-s2",
      "quote": "“Inari” is short for “Ine nari” or “Ine ni naru” (reaping of rice).",
      "locator": "Name of Deity",
      "context": "etymology",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kuil Fushimi Inari mengaitkan nama Inari dengan ungkapan lama tentang panen padi.",
        "en": "Fushimi Inari Shrine connects the name Inari with an older expression for reaping rice."
      }
    }
  ],

  "short_description": {
    "id": "Izanami adalah dewi pencipta yang bersama Izanagi membentuk pulau-pulau Jepang dan melahirkan banyak dewa.",
    "en": "Izanami is a creator goddess who forms Japan’s islands and gives birth to many deities with Izanagi.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "izanami-c01",
      "izanami-c02",
      "izanami-c03"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Izanami adalah pasangan Izanagi dalam kisah penciptaan. Mereka menciptakan pulau-pulau Jepang dan banyak dewa. Peran Izanami sebagai ibu para dewa membuatnya dikaitkan dengan citra dewi bumi.",
      "en": "Izanami is Izanagi’s partner in the creation story. Together they create Japan’s islands and many deities. Izanami’s role as mother of gods is associated with an earth-mother image.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "izanami-c01",
        "izanami-c02",
        "izanami-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Izanami meninggal setelah terbakar ketika melahirkan dewa api. Izanagi kemudian menyusulnya ke Yomi, tetapi melarikan diri setelah melihat wujudnya yang telah membusuk. Dalam kisah ini Izanami mengancam akan membunuh seribu orang setiap hari, sementara Izanagi menjawab akan melahirkan seribu lima ratus orang.",
      "en": "Izanami dies after being burned while giving birth to the fire god. Izanagi then follows her to Yomi but flees after seeing her decayed body. In this story Izanami threatens to kill a thousand people each day, while Izanagi replies that he will bring forth fifteen hundred.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "izanami-c04",
        "izanami-c05",
        "izanami-c06",
        "izanami-c07"
      ]
    }
  ],

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "izanami-c01",
      "source_id": "izanami-s1",
      "quote": "As the kami responsible for the birth of various other kami representing the land and its contents, Izanami has strong connotations of an \"earth mother\" goddess.",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Izanami adalah kami yang melahirkan dewa-dewa alam dan dikaitkan dengan citra ibu bumi.",
        "en": "Izanami is a kami who bore deities of the land and is associated with an earth-mother image."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "izanami-c02",
      "source_id": "izanami-s2",
      "quote": "Izanami ('she who invites') and Izanagi ('he who invites') are the primordial gods of the Shinto religion who are believed to have created the islands of Japan and given birth to many of the other Shinto gods or kami.",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Izanami dan Izanagi dipercaya menciptakan pulau-pulau Jepang dan melahirkan banyak dewa Shinto.",
        "en": "Izanami and Izanagi are believed to have created Japan’s islands and given birth to many Shinto gods."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "izanami-c03",
      "source_id": "izanami-s1",
      "quote": "Consort of the kami Izanagi. The name Izanami has been understood in various ways",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Izanami adalah pasangan Izanagi.",
        "en": "Izanami is Izanagi’s consort."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "izanami-c04",
      "source_id": "izanami-s1",
      "quote": "Burning her genitals as she gave birth to the god of fire, Izanami fell sick and then died",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Izanami terbakar saat melahirkan dewa api, lalu sakit dan meninggal.",
        "en": "Izanami was burned giving birth to the fire god, then fell ill and died."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "izanami-c05",
      "source_id": "izanami-s2",
      "quote": "Izanagi, no doubt unable to live without his beloved wife, rashly followed her down into the underworld or Yomi",
      "locator": "Izanami's Death & Revenge",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Izanagi menyusul Izanami ke Yomi.",
        "en": "Izanagi followed Izanami to Yomi."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "izanami-c06",
      "source_id": "izanami-s1",
      "quote": "was shocked by the putrefying appearance of his dead wife, whereupon he fled from the place",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Izanagi terkejut melihat wujud Izanami yang membusuk, lalu melarikan diri.",
        "en": "Izanagi was shocked by Izanami’s decayed appearance and fled."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "izanami-c07",
      "source_id": "izanami-s1",
      "quote": "the dead Izanami threatened to kill one thousand persons each day, but Izanagi stated that he would respond by giving birth to fifteen hundred persons",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Izanami mengancam akan membunuh seribu orang setiap hari, dan Izanagi menjawab akan melahirkan seribu lima ratus orang.",
        "en": "Izanami threatened to kill one thousand people daily, and Izanagi replied he would give birth to fifteen hundred."
      }
    }

  "traits": [],
  "short_description": {
    "id": "Yuki-onna adalah yōkai perempuan yang muncul dalam cerita bersalju di Jepang.",
    "en": "Yuki-onna is a female yōkai appearing in Japanese stories of snow.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "yuki-onna-c01",
      "yuki-onna-c02",
      "yuki-onna-c03"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Nama Yuki-onna berarti perempuan salju. Kisahnya memiliki berbagai bentuk di daerah-daerah Jepang yang bersalju. Ia kerap digambarkan sebagai perempuan berkulit sangat putih dan berpakaian putih.",
      "en": "The name Yuki-onna means snow woman. Her stories vary across Japan’s snowy regions. She is often depicted as a woman with very pale skin and white clothing.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "yuki-onna-c01",
        "yuki-onna-c02",
        "yuki-onna-c03",
        "yuki-onna-c04"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Dalam sebuah kisah, perempuan berpakaian putih meniupkan napasnya ke Mosaku. Ia membiarkan Minokichi hidup karena masih muda, dengan syarat ia tidak menceritakan pertemuan itu. Belakangan perempuan itu mengaku sebagai sosok yang dahulu ditemui Minokichi.",
      "en": "In one story, a woman in white blows her breath on Mosaku. She spares the young Minokichi on condition that he never tells anyone about the encounter. Later she reveals that she was the woman Minokichi had met.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "yuki-onna-c05",
        "yuki-onna-c06",
        "yuki-onna-c07",
        "yuki-onna-c08"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,
  "story_mode": null,
  "did_you_know": null,
  "abilities": [],
  "weaknesses": [],
  "variants": [],
  "stories": [],
  "places": [],
  "timeline": [],
  "relations": [],

  "tradition_vs_modern": null,

  "sources": [
    {
      "id": "yuki-onna-s1",
      "url": "https://hyakumonogatari.com/2013/12/18/yuki-onna-the-snow-woman/",
      "title": "Yuki Onna – The Snow Woman",

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "yuki-onna-c01",
      "source_id": "yuki-onna-s1",
      "quote": "The Yuki Onna is one of Japan’s most well-known and yet unknown yokai. There is no single story of the Yuki Onna.",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Yuki-onna adalah yōkai Jepang yang tidak mempunyai satu kisah tunggal.",
        "en": "Yuki-onna is a Japanese yōkai without a single definitive story."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yuki-onna-c02",
      "source_id": "yuki-onna-s1",
      "quote": "Yuki Onna uses the kanji 雪 (yuki; snow) + 女 (onna; woman) to mean Snow Woman.",
      "locator": "What Does Yuki Onna Mean?",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Nama Yuki-onna berarti perempuan salju.",
        "en": "The name Yuki-onna means snow woman."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yuki-onna-c03",
      "source_id": "yuki-onna-s1",
      "quote": "Anywhere there is ice and snow in Japan you will find legends of the Yuki Onna, often called by some regional variation.",
      "locator": "What Does Yuki Onna Mean?",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Cerita Yuki-onna terdapat di daerah Jepang yang bersalju dan memiliki variasi daerah.",
        "en": "Yuki-onna stories occur in snowy parts of Japan and have regional variants."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yuki-onna-c04",
      "source_id": "yuki-onna-s1",
      "quote": "She resembles a classic yurei, with snow-white skin and a white kimono. The kimono is usually described as a thin, summer kimono",
      "locator": "What Do Yuki Onna Look Like?",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Yuki-onna kerap digambarkan berkulit putih dan mengenakan kimono putih.",
        "en": "Yuki-onna is often depicted with white skin and a white kimono."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yuki-onna-c05",
      "source_id": "yuki-onna-s2",
      "quote": "he saw a woman in the room,—a woman all in white. She was bending above Mosaku, and blowing her breath upon him",
      "locator": "YUKI-ONNA",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dalam kisah Hearn, seorang perempuan berbaju putih meniupkan napasnya ke Mosaku.",
        "en": "In Hearn’s story, a woman in white blows her breath on Mosaku."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yuki-onna-c06",
      "source_id": "yuki-onna-s2",
      "quote": "But I cannot help feeling some pity for you,—because you are so young... You are a pretty boy, Minokichi; and I will not hurt you now.",
      "locator": "YUKI-ONNA",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Perempuan itu membiarkan Minokichi hidup karena usianya muda.",
        "en": "The woman spares Minokichi because he is young."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yuki-onna-c07",
      "source_id": "yuki-onna-s2",
      "quote": "But, if you ever tell anybody—even your own mother—about what you have seen this night, I shall know it; and then I will kill you",
      "locator": "YUKI-ONNA",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Perempuan itu melarang Minokichi bercerita kepada siapa pun dan mengancam akan membunuhnya jika ia melanggar.",
        "en": "The woman forbids Minokichi from telling anyone and threatens to kill him if he does."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yuki-onna-c08",
      "source_id": "yuki-onna-s2",
      "quote": "“It was I—I—I! Yuki it was! And I told you then that I would kill you if you ever said one word about it!",
      "locator": "YUKI-ONNA",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "O-Yuki mengaku sebagai perempuan salju yang ditemui Minokichi.",
        "en": "O-Yuki reveals she was the snow woman Minokichi met."
      }
    }
  ],

  "countries": null,

  "short_description": {
    "id": "Kappa adalah yōkai air dalam cerita rakyat Jepang yang dikaitkan dengan sungai dan kolam.",
    "en": "Kappa are water yōkai of Japanese folklore associated with rivers and ponds.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "kappa-folklore-c01",
      "kappa-folklore-c02",
      "kappa-folklore-c04"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Kappa dikenal sebagai makhluk air dalam cerita rakyat Jepang. Kisahnya menempatkan mereka di sungai dan kolam, tempat mereka dapat menyeret perenang ke bawah air. Cerita lain menyebut kappa gemar bergulat sumo, tetapi dapat mencelakai lawan yang kalah.",
      "en": "Kappa are water beings in Japanese folklore. Stories place them in rivers and ponds, where they may pull swimmers underwater. Other tales say kappa enjoy sumō wrestling but may harm defeated opponents.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "kappa-folklore-c01",
        "kappa-folklore-c02",
        "kappa-folklore-c04",
        "kappa-folklore-c08"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Rupa kappa berubah dalam penggambaran dari waktu ke waktu. Sebelum abad kedelapan belas, mereka sering dibayangkan seperti monyet atau berang-berang. Gambaran populer masa kini memberi mereka tubuh hijau, paruh, cekungan di kepala, dan tempurung di punggung. Sejak 1950-an, media juga kerap menampilkan kappa sebagai tokoh lucu.",
      "en": "Depictions of kappa have changed over time. Before the eighteenth century they were often imagined as monkeylike or otterlike. A common present-day image gives them green bodies, beaks, dishes on their heads, and shells on their backs. Since the 1950s, media have also often presented kappa as cute characters.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "kappa-folklore-c03",
        "kappa-folklore-c05",
        "kappa-folklore-c06",
        "kappa-folklore-c07"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,

      "author": null,

  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "kappa-folklore-c01",
      "source_id": "kappa-folklore-s1",
      "quote": "Kappa (河童 river-child) is an amphibious supernatural monster and water spirit in Japanese folklore.",
      "locator": "Page 1",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kappa adalah makhluk amfibi gaib dan roh air dalam cerita rakyat Jepang.",
        "en": "Kappa is an amphibious supernatural being and water spirit in Japanese folklore."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kappa-folklore-c02",
      "source_id": "kappa-folklore-s2",
      "quote": "Kappa are among Japan’s most famous yōkai.",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kappa termasuk yōkai terkenal di Jepang.",
        "en": "Kappa are among Japan’s well-known yōkai."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kappa-folklore-c03",
      "source_id": "kappa-folklore-s2",
      "quote": "Contemporary Japanese people typically visualize kappa as having green, hairless bodies shaped like that of a human child. They have smooth, circular dishes on their heads",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Gambaran Jepang masa kini menampilkan kappa bertubuh hijau seperti anak dan memiliki cekungan bundar di kepala.",
        "en": "A contemporary Japanese image shows kappa with green childlike bodies and round head dishes."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kappa-folklore-c04",
      "source_id": "kappa-folklore-s2",
      "quote": "Kappa live in rivers and ponds, where they catch hold of the feet of swimmers, dragging them to a watery death.",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kappa dikisahkan tinggal di sungai dan kolam serta menyeret perenang ke air.",
        "en": "Kappa are said to live in rivers and ponds and drag swimmers into the water."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kappa-folklore-c05",
      "source_id": "kappa-folklore-s2",
      "quote": "Kappa were also thought of as mammals like monkeys or otters until the eighteenth century, rather than reptiles or amphibians.",
      "locator": "A Changing Image",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Sebelum abad kedelapan belas kappa juga dipandang menyerupai monyet atau berang-berang.",
        "en": "Before the eighteenth century kappa were also seen as monkeylike or otterlike."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kappa-folklore-c06",
      "source_id": "kappa-folklore-s2",
      "quote": "it was not until the 1950s that the creatures were portrayed as straightforwardly cute.",
      "locator": "Cuter Kappa",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Sejak 1950-an kappa juga digambarkan sebagai makhluk lucu.",
        "en": "From the 1950s kappa were also portrayed as cute."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kappa-folklore-c07",
      "source_id": "kappa-folklore-s2",
      "quote": "pointed beaks like birds, which are often yellow. On their backs, they have what looks like a turtle’s shell",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Gambaran masa kini menunjukkan paruh burung dan tempurung mirip kura-kura.",
        "en": "Contemporary depictions show birdlike beaks and turtlelike shells."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "kappa-folklore-c08",
      "source_id": "kappa-folklore-s2",
      "quote": "In folklore, kappa are commonly said to enjoy sumō, and this may seem superficially charming, but not if they pull defeated humans beneath the water, or otherwise harm them.",
      "locator": "A Terrifying Past",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kappa dikisahkan gemar sumo namun dapat mencelakai lawan yang kalah.",
        "en": "Kappa are said to enjoy sumō but may harm defeated opponents."
      }
    }
  ],

  "short_description": {
    "id": "Ame no Uzume adalah dewi dalam mitologi Jepang yang tariannya membujuk Amaterasu keluar dari gua langit.",
    "en": "Ame no Uzume is a goddess in Japanese mythology whose dance drew Amaterasu out of a heavenly cave.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "ame-no-uzume-c01",
      "ame-no-uzume-c02"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Kojiki dan Nihongi mengisahkan Uzume menari di depan gua tempat Amaterasu bersembunyi. Dalam kisah itu ia mengalami kerasukan ilahi, dan tindakannya membuat para dewa tertawa sehingga Amaterasu menjadi penasaran dan keluar. Tradisi kagura mengaitkan asal tari ritualnya dengan peristiwa ini.",
      "en": "The Kojiki and Nihongi tell of Uzume dancing before the cave where Amaterasu hid. In that account she experiences divine possession, and her actions make the other deities laugh, drawing Amaterasu out in curiosity. Kagura tradition links the origin of ritual dance to this event.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "ame-no-uzume-c01",
        "ame-no-uzume-c02",
        "ame-no-uzume-c03",
        "ame-no-uzume-c04"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Uzume juga dipandang sebagai pelindung seni pertunjukan dan tokoh yang berunding dengan makhluk ilahi yang belum dikenal. Kojiki menceritakan ia diutus menemui Sarutahiko. Di Kuil Ota, ia dipuja sebagai dewi yang membantu keterampilan seni.",
      "en": "Uzume is also regarded as a patron of performing arts and a negotiator with unfamiliar divine beings. The Kojiki tells of her being sent to meet Sarutahiko. At Ota Shrine she is worshiped as a deity associated with artistic skill.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "ame-no-uzume-c05",
        "ame-no-uzume-c06",
        "ame-no-uzume-c07",
        "ame-no-uzume-c08"
      ]
    }

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "ame-no-uzume-c01",
      "source_id": "ame-no-uzume-s1",
      "quote": "The goddess who danced so as to lure Amaterasu from the heavenly rock cave.",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Uzume adalah dewi yang menari untuk menarik Amaterasu keluar dari gua langit.",
        "en": "Uzume is the goddess whose dance drew Amaterasu from the heavenly rock cave."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ame-no-uzume-c02",
      "source_id": "ame-no-uzume-s2",
      "quote": "According to Japanese mythology, the origins of kagura lie with a dance performed by the Goddess Ame-no-uzume-no-mikoto to bring out Amaterasu Oomikami",
      "locator": "The beginnings of kagura",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dalam mitologi Jepang, tari Uzume yang memanggil Amaterasu dikaitkan dengan asal kagura.",
        "en": "In Japanese mythology, Uzume’s dance to draw out Amaterasu is linked with kagura’s origin."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ame-no-uzume-c03",
      "source_id": "ame-no-uzume-s1",
      "quote": "Both Nihongi and Kojiki relate that after Amaterasu hid away in the rock cave of heaven, Amenouzume underwent divine possession (kamigakari)",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kojiki dan Nihongi menyebut Uzume mengalami kerasukan ilahi setelah Amaterasu bersembunyi.",
        "en": "The Kojiki and Nihongi say Uzume experienced divine possession after Amaterasu hid."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ame-no-uzume-c04",
      "source_id": "ame-no-uzume-s1",
      "quote": "This behavior drew amused exclamations from the other assembled kami, and attracted the curiosity of Amaterasu, who at last came out from the cave.",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Tindakan Uzume membuat para kami tertawa dan membangkitkan rasa ingin tahu Amaterasu hingga ia keluar dari gua.",
        "en": "Uzume’s actions made the kami laugh and drew Amaterasu out of the cave in curiosity."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ame-no-uzume-c05",
      "source_id": "ame-no-uzume-s1",
      "quote": "In addition to her role as patron kami of actors and other performing arts, Uzume is also viewed as having the role of negotiator with new, unknown beings.",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Uzume dipandang sebagai pelindung seni pertunjukan dan perunding dengan makhluk yang belum dikenal.",
        "en": "Uzume is regarded as a patron of performing arts and a negotiator with unfamiliar beings."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ame-no-uzume-c06",
      "source_id": "ame-no-uzume-s1",
      "quote": "sent her to confront and ascertain the identity of the kami Sarutahiko",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Uzume diutus untuk menemui dan mengenali Sarutahiko.",
        "en": "Uzume was sent to meet and identify Sarutahiko."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ame-no-uzume-c07",
      "source_id": "ame-no-uzume-s3",
      "quote": "Ota Jinja Shrine is located approximately 800 meters to the east of Kamigamo Jinja grounds and is dedicated to Ame no Uzume no Mikoto",
      "locator": "Ota Jinja Shrine",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kuil Ota didedikasikan kepada Ame no Uzume.",
        "en": "Ota Shrine is dedicated to Ame no Uzume."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ame-no-uzume-c08",
      "source_id": "ame-no-uzume-s3",
      "quote": "Ame no Uzume no Mikoto is primarily worshipped as a deity that helps improve artistic skills.",
      "locator": "Ota Jinja Shrine",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ame no Uzume dipuja sebagai dewi yang membantu keterampilan seni.",
        "en": "Ame no Uzume is worshiped as a deity associated with artistic skill."
      }
    }

  "traits": [],
  "short_description": {
    "id": "Hachiman adalah kami Jepang yang dipuja sebagai dewa perang dan pelindung kalangan prajurit.",
    "en": "Hachiman is a kami of Japan revered as a war deity and protector of warriors.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "hachiman-c01",
      "hachiman-c02",
      "hachiman-c07"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Dalam istilah Shinto, Hachiman berkaitan dengan pemuliaan Kaisar Ōjin bersama Jingū dan Himegami. Pemujaannya mula-mula berpusat di Usa Hachimangū, kemudian menyebar ke banyak kuil di Jepang. Kaum militer secara historis memujanya sebagai dewa perang.",
      "en": "In Shinto terminology, Hachiman is associated with the deified Emperor Ōjin together with Empress Jingū and Himegami. Worship first centered at Usa Hachimangū and later spread to many shrines in Japan. The military class historically revered Hachiman as a war deity.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "hachiman-c02",
        "hachiman-c03"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Hachiman juga menjadi contoh pertemuan tradisi Shinto dan Buddha: ia mendapat gelar Buddha Daibosatsu. Klan Minamoto menghormatinya sebagai pelindung khusus, dan para samurai memujanya sebagai pelindung pribadi dalam masa konflik.",
      "en": "Hachiman also illustrates the meeting of Shinto and Buddhist traditions: he received the Buddhist title Daibosatsu. The Minamoto clan revered him as a special patron, and samurai worshiped him as a personal protector in times of conflict.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "hachiman-c01",
        "hachiman-c04",
        "hachiman-c05",
        "hachiman-c06",
        "hachiman-c07"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,

  "story_mode": null,

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "hachiman-c01",
      "source_id": "hachiman-s1",
      "quote": "Hachiman, one of the principal Shintō deities (kami)",
      "locator": "Object description",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Hachiman adalah salah satu kami utama dalam Shinto.",
        "en": "Hachiman is one of the principal Shinto kami."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "hachiman-c02",
      "source_id": "hachiman-s2",
      "quote": "Historically worshiped by the military class as a god of war, Hachiman is now the object of deep devotion for many people in Japan.",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kaum militer memuja Hachiman sebagai dewa perang di Jepang.",
        "en": "The military class historically worshiped Hachiman as a war deity in Japan."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "hachiman-c03",
      "source_id": "hachiman-s2",
      "quote": "Generally refers to the deified Emperor Ôjin together with his mother Empress Jingû and his wife Himegami. They were first enshrined in the Usa Hachimangû in Oita Prefecture and later in many Hachiman shrines throughout Japan.",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Hachiman berkaitan dengan Kaisar Ōjin yang didewakan bersama Jingū dan Himegami; mereka mula-mula dipuja di Usa Hachimangū lalu di banyak kuil Jepang.",
        "en": "Hachiman is associated with deified Emperor Ōjin, Jingū, and Himegami, first enshrined at Usa Hachimangū and later at many Japanese shrines."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "hachiman-c04",
      "source_id": "hachiman-s1",
      "quote": "was among the first indigenous gods assimilated into the Buddhist pantheon in Japan.",
      "locator": "Object description",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Hachiman termasuk dewa lokal awal yang dimasukkan ke dalam lingkungan Buddha di Jepang.",
        "en": "Hachiman was among the early indigenous gods assimilated into the Buddhist pantheon in Japan."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "hachiman-c05",
      "source_id": "hachiman-s1",
      "quote": "By the late eighth century he was officially accorded the Buddhist title Daibosatsu (Great Bodhisattva).",
      "locator": "Object description",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Hachiman memperoleh gelar Buddha Daibosatsu.",
        "en": "Hachiman received the Buddhist title Daibosatsu."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "hachiman-c06",
      "source_id": "hachiman-s1",
      "quote": "as the particular patron of the Minamoto clan, founders of the first shogunate",
      "locator": "Object description",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Hachiman adalah pelindung khusus klan Minamoto.",
        "en": "Hachiman was a particular patron of the Minamoto clan."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "hachiman-c07",
      "source_id": "hachiman-s1",
      "quote": "Hachiman was considered the protector of the state in times of strife and was revered as a personal protector by individual samurai of all ranks.",
      "locator": "Object description",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Hachiman dianggap pelindung negara saat konflik dan pelindung pribadi para samurai.",
        "en": "Hachiman was regarded as protector of the state in conflict and personal protector of samurai."
      }
    }

  "short_description": {
    "id": "Yūrei adalah roh orang meninggal dalam tradisi Jepang yang dapat tetap berada di dunia manusia.",
    "en": "Yūrei are spirits of deceased people in Japanese tradition that may remain in the human world.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "yurei-c01",
      "yurei-c02",
      "yurei-c03"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Yūrei merujuk kepada roh orang yang telah meninggal. Sebagian kisah menggambarkan roh itu belum menyeberang ke alam lain karena alasan pribadi atau karena upacara pemakaman yang dianggap kurang memadai. Dalam seni dan sastra, gambaran yang dikenal ialah perempuan berambut hitam panjang, berpakaian pemakaman, dengan tangan menggantung dan tanpa kaki.",
      "en": "Yūrei refers to the spirits of deceased people. Some accounts describe them as remaining in the human world for personal reasons or because funeral rites were considered insufficient. In art and literature, a familiar image is a long-haired woman in burial clothes, with dangling hands and no feet.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "yurei-c01",
        "yurei-c03",
        "yurei-c04"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Makna istilah ini berubah dalam sejarah Jepang. Pada masa kuno dan pertengahan yūrei lazim berarti jiwa orang mati dan belum dipandang dapat mengutuk. Sekitar abad kelima belas, istilah itu mulai dikaitkan dengan roh yang menyimpan dendam. Pada zaman Edo, cerita hantu, kabuki, dan seni cetak turut menampilkan sosok yūrei yang menakutkan.",
      "en": "The term changed meaning through Japanese history. In ancient and medieval usage yūrei usually meant the souls of the dead and were not thought able to curse. Around the fifteenth century the word began to describe resentful spirits. In the Edo period, ghost tales, kabuki, and prints helped present frightening yūrei.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "yurei-c02",
        "yurei-c05",
        "yurei-c06",
        "yurei-c07",
        "yurei-c08",
        "yurei-c09"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,
  "story_mode": null,
  "did_you_know": null,
  "abilities": [],
  "weaknesses": [],
  "variants": [],
  "stories": [],
  "places": [],

  "tradition_vs_modern": null,

    {
      "id": "yurei-s1",

    {
      "id": "yurei-s2",
      "url": "https://www.nippon.com/en/japan-topics/g02176/",
      "title": "Fear and Reverence: Japanese Views of Souls, Spirits, and Ghosts",
      "author": "Kimie Itakura",
      "publisher": "Nippon.com",
      "published": "2022-08-30",
      "language": "en",
      "type": "other",
      "accessed": "2026-09-30"
    }
  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "yurei-c01",
      "source_id": "yurei-s1",
      "quote": "Generally speaking, a yūrei is the spirit of a person who has died, usually appearing as they did in life.",
      "locator": "Yūrei",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Yūrei adalah roh seseorang yang sudah meninggal.",
        "en": "Yūrei is the spirit of a deceased person."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yurei-c02",
      "source_id": "yurei-s2",
      "quote": "The Japanese word yūrei conjures up an image today of a spiteful ghost, but its meaning has changed considerably over the centuries.",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Makna kata yūrei dalam bahasa Jepang berubah selama berabad-abad.",
        "en": "The meaning of the Japanese word yūrei changed over the centuries."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yurei-c03",
      "source_id": "yurei-s1",
      "quote": "Yūrei refers to the spirits of humans who do not cross over to the other side and remain in the human world for personal reasons or because the funeral or burial rites were not sufficient or satisfactory.",
      "locator": "Yūrei",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Yūrei dapat tinggal di dunia manusia karena alasan pribadi atau upacara pemakaman yang kurang memadai.",
        "en": "Yūrei may remain in the human world for personal reasons or insufficient funeral rites."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yurei-c04",
      "source_id": "yurei-s1",
      "quote": "The typical yūrei, as depicted in art and literature, is described as a woman with long, disheveled black hair obstructing the face, wearing burial clothing, with curved dangling hands and no feet.",
      "locator": "Yūrei",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dalam seni dan sastra, yūrei lazim digambarkan sebagai perempuan berambut hitam panjang, berpakaian pemakaman, bertangan menggantung, dan tanpa kaki.",
        "en": "In art and literature yūrei are often shown as long-haired women in burial clothes, with dangling hands and no feet."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yurei-c05",
      "source_id": "yurei-s2",
      "quote": "In ancient and medieval Japan, when there was considerable “commerce” between the people of this world and spirits, the word yūrei typically referred to the souls of the dead, who neither showed themselves nor enacted curses.",
      "locator": "Introduction",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Di Jepang kuno dan pertengahan, yūrei biasanya berarti jiwa orang mati yang tidak menampakkan diri atau mengutuk.",
        "en": "In ancient and medieval Japan, yūrei usually meant souls of the dead who neither appeared nor cursed."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yurei-c06",
      "source_id": "yurei-s2",
      "quote": "Around the fifteenth century, the word yūrei began to shift from describing the souls of the memorialized dead to represent spirits harboring a grudge.",
      "locator": "Fear as Entertainment",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Sekitar abad kelima belas, yūrei mulai berarti roh yang menyimpan dendam.",
        "en": "Around the fifteenth century, yūrei began to mean resentful spirits."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yurei-c07",
      "source_id": "yurei-s2",
      "quote": "In the Edo period (1603–1868), there was a belief that a bad relationship with someone in life could lead to that person taking revenge after death.",
      "locator": "Fear as Entertainment",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Pada zaman Edo, hubungan buruk semasa hidup dipercaya dapat berujung pada pembalasan setelah kematian.",
        "en": "In the Edo period, people believed a bad relationship in life could lead to revenge after death."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yurei-c08",
      "source_id": "yurei-s2",
      "quote": "In the later Edo period, it was common for yūrei to appear in kabuki, providing inspiration for ukiyo-e artists.",
      "locator": "Fear as Entertainment",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Pada akhir zaman Edo, yūrei kerap muncul dalam kabuki dan mengilhami seniman ukiyo-e.",
        "en": "In the later Edo period, yūrei appeared in kabuki and inspired ukiyo-e artists."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yurei-c09",
      "source_id": "yurei-s2",
      "quote": "there was a craze for kaidan (ghost stories) and many artists painted pictures of yūrei.",
      "locator": "Fear as Entertainment",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Pada zaman Edo, cerita hantu menjadi populer dan seniman melukis yūrei.",
        "en": "In the Edo period, ghost stories became popular and artists painted yūrei."
      }
    }
  ],
  "conflicts": [],

    {

  ]
}

    "en": "Benzaiten is a goddess worshiped in Japan, derived from Sarasvatī and associated with music, eloquence, and fortune.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "benzaiten-c01",
      "benzaiten-c02",
      "benzaiten-c03"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Benzaiten berasal dari Sarasvatī, dewi air India. Di Jepang ia dikenal sebagai pelindung musik dan kefasihan, serta termasuk dalam tujuh dewa keberuntungan. Ia juga masuk ke dalam tradisi Shinto dan Buddha. Pada masa Muromachi, kelompok tujuh dewa keberuntungan mulai dikenal di Jepang.",
      "en": "Benzaiten derives from Sarasvatī, an Indian water goddess. In Japan she is a patron of music and eloquence and is counted among the Seven Gods of Good Fortune. She was also adopted into Shinto and Buddhist traditions. The grouping of seven fortune deities emerged in Japan during the Muromachi period.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "benzaiten-c01",
        "benzaiten-c02",
        "benzaiten-c03",
        "benzaiten-c04"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Pemujaan Benzaiten mengambil bentuk khas di Jepang. Ia berpadu dengan Ugajin menjadi Uga Benten; penggambaran ini dapat memiliki delapan lengan dan ular putih berkepala manusia di atas kepalanya. Bentuk itu berbeda dari gambaran Benzaiten dalam sutra lain. Pemujaan Uga Benzaiten menyebar dari Chikubujima ke tempat seperti Itsukushima, Enoshima, dan Tenkawa.",
      "en": "Worship of Benzaiten developed distinctive forms in Japan. She merged with Ugajin as Uga Benten, a form portrayed with eight arms and a white snake with a human face on her head. This differs from other sutra depictions of Benzaiten. Worship of Uga Benzaiten spread from Chikubujima to places including Itsukushima, Enoshima, and Tenkawa.",

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "benzaiten-c01",
      "source_id": "benzaiten-s1",
      "quote": "Benzaiten was originally the Indian goddess of water, Sarasvatī, and is known in Japan as a patron tutelary of music and eloquence (wisdom).",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Benzaiten berasal dari Sarasvatī, dewi air India, dan di Jepang melindungi musik serta kefasihan.",
        "en": "Benzaiten derives from the Indian water goddess Sarasvatī and in Japan is a patron of music and eloquence."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "benzaiten-c02",
      "source_id": "benzaiten-s2",
      "quote": "Benzaiten originated from the Hindu goddess Saraswati, and has been adopted into the pantheons of both Shinto and Buddhism.",
      "locator": "Page 1",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Benzaiten berasal dari Saraswati dan masuk dalam tradisi Shinto serta Buddha.",
        "en": "Benzaiten derives from Saraswati and was adopted into Shinto and Buddhist pantheons."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "benzaiten-c03",
      "source_id": "benzaiten-s1",
      "quote": "\"Seven deities of good fortune,\" seven deities reputed to bring good luck. Most commonly, the seven include Ebisu, Daikoku, Bishamonten, Fukurokuju, Jurōjin, Benzaiten, and Hōtei",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Benzaiten termasuk tujuh dewa keberuntungan di Jepang.",
        "en": "Benzaiten is among the Seven Gods of Good Fortune."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "benzaiten-c04",
      "source_id": "benzaiten-s1",
      "quote": "The cult of seven deities of good fortune is believed to have originated in Japan's Muromachi period",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kelompok tujuh dewa keberuntungan diyakini bermula pada masa Muromachi di Jepang.",
        "en": "The grouping of seven fortune deities is believed to have begun in Japan’s Muromachi period."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "benzaiten-c05",
      "source_id": "benzaiten-s3",
      "quote": "Fused with the Buddhist deity Benzaiten, the kami became known as Uga Benten",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ugajin berpadu dengan Benzaiten menjadi Uga Benten.",
        "en": "Ugajin merged with Benzaiten as Uga Benten."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "benzaiten-c06",
      "source_id": "benzaiten-s3",
      "quote": "Their contents depict the deity in the form of a heavenly woman with eight arms",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Bentuk Uga Benten digambarkan sebagai perempuan langit berlengan delapan.",
        "en": "Uga Benten is portrayed as a heavenly woman with eight arms."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "benzaiten-c07",
      "source_id": "benzaiten-s3",
      "quote": "A white snake with the face of an elderly human rests on her head",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Seekor ular putih berkepala manusia tua berada di kepalanya.",
        "en": "A white snake with an elderly human face rests on her head."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "benzaiten-c08",
      "source_id": "benzaiten-s3",
      "quote": "These depictions differ greatly from the conventional portrait of Benzaiten found in the sutras Dainichikyō and Saishōō-kyō, and reflect a unique development of the Benten cult in Japan. From Ōmi Chikubujima near Mt. Hiei, the cult of Uga Benzaiten spread throughout Japan, including Itsukushima in Aki, Enoshima in Sagami, and Tenkawa in Kii",
      "locator": "Complete Article",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Gambaran Uga Benten berbeda dari sutra lain; pemujaannya menyebar dari Chikubujima ke Itsukushima, Enoshima, dan Tenkawa.",
        "en": "Uga Benten differs from other sutra depictions; worship spread from Chikubujima to Itsukushima, Enoshima, and Tenkawa."
      }
    }
  ],

  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Kojiki memuat kisah Yamata no Orochi, ular raksasa yang namanya berarti ular bercabang delapan. Ia digambarkan memiliki delapan kepala dan delapan ekor, mata merah, serta tubuh yang membentang melintasi delapan gunung dan lembah. Dalam kisah Izumo, ular itu telah memakan putri-putri sepasang orang tua dan akan memangsa Kushinada-hime.",
      "en": "The Kojiki tells of Yamata no Orochi, a giant serpent whose name means eight-forked snake. It has eight heads and tails, red eyes, and a body spanning eight mountains and valleys. In the Izumo story it has eaten an elderly couple’s daughters and is about to devour Kushinada-hime.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "yamata-no-orochi-c01",
        "yamata-no-orochi-c02",
        "yamata-no-orochi-c03",
        "yamata-no-orochi-c04",
        "yamata-no-orochi-c09"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Susanoo menjebak Orochi dengan arak beras, lalu membunuhnya ketika mabuk. Dari salah satu ekornya ia menemukan pedang Kusanagi dan mempersembahkannya kepada Amaterasu; pedang itu kemudian menjadi salah satu pusaka kekaisaran. Sebuah tafsir melihat Orochi sebagai lambang Sungai Hii dan penaklukannya sebagai penjinakan aliran air untuk irigasi, tetapi ini adalah tafsir atas mitos, bukan identitas yang pasti.",
      "en": "Susanoo traps Orochi with rice wine and kills it after it becomes drunk. He finds the sword Kusanagi in a tail and presents it to Amaterasu; the sword later becomes an imperial regalia treasure. One interpretation reads Orochi as the Hii River and the defeat as a story about taming water for irrigation, but that is an interpretation rather than an established identity.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "yamata-no-orochi-c04",
        "yamata-no-orochi-c05",
        "yamata-no-orochi-c06",
        "yamata-no-orochi-c07",
        "yamata-no-orochi-c08"
      ]
    }
